| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[Sharp Volume Contraction of a Centrally Cut John Ellipsoid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29323v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ in positive dimension, let $w(K)$ be the volume of its maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. For a closed halfspace $H$ whose boundary passes through the center of that ellipsoid, we prove \[ w(K\cap H)\le R_nw(K),\qquad R_1=\frac12,\qquad R_n=\frac12\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}\quad(n\ge2), \] with equality attained in every fixed dimension. We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |
| **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. We present ADEPT, a framework that adapts these settings using learned feedback from a frozen depth model. During training, ground-truth depth identifies the lowest-error reference within a group of physically evaluated camera settings. A compact predictor learns reference-relative disagreement (RRD) between each candidate's depth prediction and the reference prediction using regression and ranking losses. At deployment, it scores observed captures using frozen-model features and camera, motion, and illumination context, without ground truth or reference images. A contextual Gaussian-process controller uses recent scores to select subsequent exposure and gain settings under motion and photometric constraints. We evaluate offline camera selection with relative and metric Depth Anything v2 and demonstrate online control with the affine-invariant variant on a mobile robot. Across nine equally weighted scene-illumination conditions, ADEPT reduces mean scale-and-shift-aligned $\mathrm{Abs}_{\mathrm{rel}}$ by 14.6% relative to built-in auto-exposure and by 10.0% relative to the best-performing evaluated prior controller.</p></details> |  |
| **[Improved Accuracy Scaling with Problem Complexity in LLM Arithmetic Reasoning Using Executable Symbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27203v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High accuracy on mathematical benchmarks does not establish whether reasoning remains reliable as problem complexity increases. We examine how executable symbolic representations change accuracy scaling in large language models compared with natural-language reasoning. To address this limitation, we introduce the GSM Arithmetic Benchmark (GSM-AB), a controlled benchmark that independently varies operand digit length and operation count to probe numerical scale and compositional complexity. We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. A Python program-of-thought (Python-PoT) control shows a similar operation-axis pattern, suggesting that this finding is not specific to Lean. These findings show that reasoning representation shapes arithmetic accuracy scaling and support executable symbolic representations for more reliable reasoning on complex arithmetic tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Indyk and Xu introduced the notion of $α$-reachable graphs, which include graphs built by DiskANN's slow-preprocessing construction, and proved that greedy search achieves an approximation factor of $\tfrac{α+1}{α-1}$. Independently, Al-Jazzazi et al. proposed adaptive beam search (ABS) with a relaxation parameter $γ&gt; 0$, showing a $\tfrac{2}γ$ approximation on navigable graphs ($α\approx 1$), where greedy search admits no guarantees. In this work, we unify and generalize these results by proving that ABS with parameter $γ$ on $α$-reachable graphs achieves an approximation of $\tfrac{α+1}{α(γ+1)-1}$ for any metric. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. In memory, it needs $1.3$--$23.7\%$ fewer distance computations than CBS and $0.0$--$3.9\%$ fewer than ABS.</p></details> | 22 pages, 1 figure, 9 tables |
| **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |

## Gaussian importance
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Deterministic Verification and Computation of Real Matrix Products Have the Same Arithmetic Exponent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34814v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given three $n\times n$ real matrices $A,B,C$, we ask whether $AB=C$. In an exact real arithmetic model that charges one step for each addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, comparison, or sign test, we prove that deterministic exact verification requires $Ω(n^{ω_{\mathbb{C}}})$ steps and has the same optimal arithmetic exponent $ω_{\mathbb{C}}$ as matrix multiplication. Assuming the value $ω_{\mathbb{C}}=9/4$ and the fixed-size border-rank lower bound stated in the OpenAI preprint of November 24, 2026, deterministic verification requires $Ω(n^{9/4})$ steps, whereas Freivalds' randomized test uses $O(n^2)$ steps with one-sided error. More generally, if a tree of cost $s$ correctly decides $y=T(u,v)$ on all real inputs, the bilinear map $T$ is a coefficientwise limit of tensors of real rank $O(s)$.</p></details> | The core proof was developed using generative AI. See the manuscript for details. Independent verification and collaboration on a revised version are welcome |
| **[Complexity of Autobidding Equilibria in GSP Sponsored Shopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13716v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We revisit autobidding equilibria in GSP sponsored shopping auctions, recently proposed and studied in Dütting et al. We establish computational upper bounds for both budget constraints (ABE-B) and return-on-investment constraints (ABE-R): computing an exact equilibrium is in FIXP, while computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is in PPAD even for inverse-exponentially small $γ$. At the same time, we establish tight inapproximability for both models: for every fixed $0\leqγ&lt;1$, computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is PPAD-hard, even when all items are displayed and all click-through rates are equal. A key ingredient in our hardness reductions is a new variant of PureCircuit with bounded forward span, which we prove remains PPAD-hard. This structural restriction makes our reduction parameters independent of circuit size, enabling hardness for every fixed $γ&lt;1$. More broadly, our approach suggests a strategy for strengthening PPAD-hardness results in other settings, by developing structural insights into the target problem and new hardness-preserving restrictions on PureCircuit in tandem, with each informing the other and the design of the reduction.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Much Effort Does an AI Code Reviewer Need? Evidence from Claude 5.5 Models on Plausible-but-Wrong Patches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33639v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI coding agents write code faster than people can review it, so teams add AI reviewers. Reasoning models let such a reviewer trade cost for diligence through an effort setting. We asked how much effort a reviewer needs to catch plausible-but-wrong patches, which pass a benchmark's tests but fail stronger ones. Claude Opus 5.5 (preregistered), Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 (exploratory) each reviewed the same 90 agent-written Django patches from SWE-bench Verified at low, medium and high effort. By strengthened SWE-ABS tests, 55 patches were wrong and 35 correct. In every model, high effort accepted fewer wrong patches than low (false success .45 against .67 for Opus, .49 against .65 for Sonnet, .51 against .78 for Haiku), a supported difference for Opus and Haiku. For Opus, medium was non-inferior to high within 15 percentage points (difference .018, 95% CI [-.065, .108]) and cost 20% less, short of the preregistered 30%. For Sonnet and Haiku, medium cost 39% and 51% less than high but was not shown non-inferior. Opus at medium or high agreed best with the labels; Haiku at high cost about 8% as much per review as Opus at medium, accuracy difference unresolved. Exploratory coding showed that most rejections named the tested defect and that effort added such rejections. No setting is a strong gate: each accepted at least 45% of wrong patches. For 32 of the 55 wrong patches the issue may not require what the failing test checks; without them, neither accuracy hypothesis holds for any model.</p></details> | 18 pages, 7 figures. Preregistered at https://osf.io/r8q2m. Data and code: https://github.com/trustmybot/research-artifacts |
| **[Patient-Zero: Scaling Synthetic Patient Agents to Real-World Distributions without Real Patient Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11078v3)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synthetic data generation with Large Language Models (LLMs) has emerged as a promising solution in the medical domain to mitigate data scarcity and privacy constraints. However, existing approaches remain constrained by their derivative nature, relying on real-world records, which pose privacy risks and distribution biases. Furthermore, current patient agents face the Stability-Plasticity Dilemma, struggling to maintain clinical consistency during dynamic inquiries. To address these challenges, we introduce Patient-Zero, a novel framework for ab initio patient simulation that requires no real medical records. Our Medically-Aligned Hierarchical Synthesis framework generates comprehensive and diverse patient records from medical knowledge via stratified attribute permutation. To support rigorous clinical interaction, we design a Dual-Track Cognitive Memory System to enable agents dynamically update memory while preserving logical consistency and persona adherence. Extensive evaluations show that Patient-Zero outperforms baselines in both data quality and interaction fidelity. In physician evaluations, our records obtain the highest mean diversity, consistency, and completeness ratings. Downstream medical reasoning model trained on our synthetic dataset shows substantial performance gains (MedQA +24.0%; MMLU +14.5%), demonstrating the practical utility of our framework.</p></details> | 27 pages, 5 figures. Published in EMNLP 2026 |
| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |

For a closed halfspace $H$ whose boundary passes through the center of that ellipsoid, we prove \[ w(K\cap H)\le R_nw(K),\qquad R_1=\frac12,\qquad R_n=\frac12\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}\quad(n\ge2), \] with equality attained in every fixed dimension. We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |

By integrating learning-enabled probabilistic uncertainty modeling into the parallel estimation structure, the proposed method achieves uncertainty-aware scal- able voltage estimation. Case studies on two unbalanced IEEE distribution networks show that the proposed estimator achieves significantly higher accuracy and computational efficiency under unknown uncertainty, outperforming recent data-driven and model-based methods.</p></details> |  |
| **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. | **[Improved Accuracy Scaling with Problem Complexity in LLM Arithmetic Reasoning Using Executable Symbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27203v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High accuracy on mathematical benchmarks does not establish whether reasoning remains reliable as problem complexity increases. To address this limitation, we introduce the GSM Arithmetic Benchmark (GSM-AB), a controlled benchmark that independently varies operand digit length and operation count to probe numerical scale and compositional complexity. We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. | **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Indyk and Xu introduced the notion of $α$-reachable graphs, which include graphs built by DiskANN's slow-preprocessing construction, and proved that greedy search achieves an approximation factor of $\tfrac{α+1}{α-1}$. Independently, Al-Jazzazi et al. proposed adaptive beam search (ABS) with a relaxation parameter $γ&gt; 0$, showing a $\tfrac{2}γ$ approximation on navigable graphs ($α\approx 1$), where greedy search admits no guarantees. In this work, we unify and generalize these results by proving that ABS with parameter $γ$ on $α$-reachable graphs achieves an approximation of $\tfrac{α+1}{α(γ+1)-1}$ for any metric. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. | **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |

## feed-forward 3D reconstruction
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Deterministic Verification and Computation of Real Matrix Products Have the Same Arithmetic Exponent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34814v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given three $n\times n$ real matrices $A,B,C$, we ask whether $AB=C$. In an exact real arithmetic model that charges one step for each addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, comparison, or sign test, we prove that deterministic exact verification requires $Ω(n^{ω_{\mathbb{C}}})$ steps and has the same optimal arithmetic exponent $ω_{\mathbb{C}}$ as matrix multiplication. Assuming the value $ω_{\mathbb{C}}=9/4$ and the fixed-size border-rank lower bound stated in the OpenAI preprint of November 24, 2026, deterministic verification requires $Ω(n^{9/4})$ steps, whereas Freivalds' randomized test uses $O(n^2)$ steps with one-sided error. More generally, if a tree of cost $s$ correctly decides $y=T(u,v)$ on all real inputs, the bilinear map $T$ is a coefficientwise limit of tensors of real rank $O(s)$.</p></details> | The core proof was developed using generative AI. See the manuscript for details. Independent verification and collaboration on a revised version are welcome |
| **[Who Is Ego? Scaling Cross-View Camera Wearer Identification and Segmentation beyond Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34489v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A first-person (ego) image reveals what a camera sees, but not who or what carries it. Given a synchronized third-person (exo) image, camera wearer identification asks who is ego: which candidate carries the ego camera? We extend this task beyond humans to robots, vehicles, animals, and drones, and broaden its spatial scope (where is ego) from selecting a provided candidate mask to wearer segmentation with absence prediction and mutual camera localization across ego-exo views. We introduce FindEgo-1M, comprising 1.16M real and simulated ego-exo pairs across 12 wearer types, 17 domains, and 111 test settings. Our approach exploits a geometric relation: the ego camera's projection into the exo image, i.e., the epipole, lies on or near its wearer. We introduce EgoRay-ZS, the first method to turn a frozen feed-forward 3D reconstruction model, VGGT-$Ω$, into a training-free solution for wearer identification and segmentation. Its geometric readout ranks candidate masks or segmentation proposals by angular offset and distance to the ego camera, while the relative pose provides mutual camera directions. We then develop the full EgoRay model through pose-supervised training on FindEgo-1M and label-free test-time adaptation constrained by candidate masks. Without task-specific training, EgoRay-ZS achieves 78.0% identification accuracy on TF2023's original labels, versus 52.9% reported for the dataset-trained PEN. On FindEgo-1M, EgoRay achieves state-of-the-art chance-corrected accuracy of 87.1 and 77.7 on seen and unseen settings. On matched queries, EgoRay also outperforms GPT-6 Astra by 31.6 and 21.3 points on seen and unseen settings, respectively, while running $18\times$ faster. Data, code, and models will be released.</p></details> |  |
| **[Complexity of Autobidding Equilibria in GSP Sponsored Shopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13716v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We revisit autobidding equilibria in GSP sponsored shopping auctions, recently proposed and studied in Dütting et al. At the same time, we establish tight inapproximability for both models: for every fixed $0\leqγ&lt;1$, computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is PPAD-hard, even when all items are displayed and all click-through rates are equal. A key ingredient in our hardness reductions is a new variant of PureCircuit with bounded forward span, which we prove remains PPAD-hard. | **[Free Geometry: Refining 3D Reconstruction from Stronger Versions of Itself](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.14048v2)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward 3D reconstruction models are efficient but rigid: once trained, they run zero-shot and cannot adapt to the test scene. Our key insight is that a model can become its own teacher at test time. When given more views, the model produces more geometrically consistent reconstructions. Aggregating multiple such predictions across different reference frames and view subsets further improves their accuracy and reliability, forming a stronger consensus teacher. Leveraging this property, given a test sequence, we mask different subsets of it to construct multiple partial observations. Free Geometry then adapts the model by enforcing camera pose and depth consistency between these partial-view predictions and the teacher's consensus. By updating only the normalization layers, this self-supervision enables fast scene-specific adaptation in about two minutes on a single GPU. Our approach improves five state-of-the-art foundation models on four datasets, yielding average relative gains of 8.8% in camera pose accuracy (AUC@3) and 3.9% in point map accuracy (inlier ratio). | **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. | **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. | **[SGAR: Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction via Sparse Geometry-Guided Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26493v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward models have been very successful in 3D reconstruction with ad hoc attention designs that relate all view patches densely. Thus, dense global attention is replaced by much more efficient geometric-similarity-guided sparse attention. SGAR is a general method that can be applied to different feed-forward models by fine-tuning. In our experiments, we show that SGAR is the fastest among both state-of-the-art dense and sparse methods and the most accurate sparse one. It runs up to 12-times faster than dense attention at 1,000 views with stable pose accuracy where existing sparse methods fail, and shows the best performance in reconstructing more complete geometries on the same time budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |

## feed-forward Gaussian Splatting
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Deterministic Verification and Computation of Real Matrix Products Have the Same Arithmetic Exponent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34814v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given three $n\times n$ real matrices $A,B,C$, we ask whether $AB=C$. In an exact real arithmetic model that charges one step for each addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, comparison, or sign test, we prove that deterministic exact verification requires $Ω(n^{ω_{\mathbb{C}}})$ steps and has the same optimal arithmetic exponent $ω_{\mathbb{C}}$ as matrix multiplication. Assuming the value $ω_{\mathbb{C}}=9/4$ and the fixed-size border-rank lower bound stated in the OpenAI preprint of November 24, 2026, deterministic verification requires $Ω(n^{9/4})$ steps, whereas Freivalds' randomized test uses $O(n^2)$ steps with one-sided error. More generally, if a tree of cost $s$ correctly decides $y=T(u,v)$ on all real inputs, the bilinear map $T$ is a coefficientwise limit of tensors of real rank $O(s)$.</p></details> | The core proof was developed using generative AI. See the manuscript for details. Independent verification and collaboration on a revised version are welcome |
| **[Feed-Forward Adaptive Gaussian Splatting for Interactive Medical Volume Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34478v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Path-traced rendering shows CT and MR volumes with realistic lighting, but it restarts after every change of view, clipping plane or transfer function (TF), the map from voxel intensity to colour and opacity, which limits the frame rate. Feed-forward Gaussian splatting models such as RenderFM predict the Gaussians directly from the volume instead of fitting them to each scan, and are fast. RenderFM, however, handles only CT, runs its network again after every TF edit, has no clipping operator, and places its Gaussians on a uniform 3-mm grid that blurs organ boundaries and cut faces. We present RenderFM-Next, a feed-forward model that addresses these limits. One network handles both CT and MR. Its Gaussians are predicted once per volume: a new TF needs no pass of this network, and a clipping plane cuts them exactly, using operators from prior work. The model predicts coarse 3-mm Gaussians for the whole volume and refines them to 1.5 mm only where refinement improves the image. A small score head ranks the coarse Gaussians by how much refining them would reduce the rendering error; at render time the user chooses how many are refined, and those next to a clipping plane are always refined, which sharpens cut faces. We build a benchmark of about 2,000 CT and MR scans with path-traced references under clinical presets, TF edits and clipping. Our RenderFM-Next improves over RenderFM retrained on this benchmark by 2.8 dB on CT and 2.4 dB on MR and renders a 1600x1600 view in 4-6 ms. Refinement adds 0.30 / 0.69 dB over the same model trained without it (0.51 / 1.17 dB on cut faces), and refining a learned 10% of the coarse Gaussians matches a random 50% with 2.4x fewer Gaussians. Project page: https://gaozhongpai.github.io/renderfmnext/</p></details> |  |
| **[Complexity of Autobidding Equilibria in GSP Sponsored Shopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13716v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We revisit autobidding equilibria in GSP sponsored shopping auctions, recently proposed and studied in Dütting et al. We establish computational upper bounds for both budget constraints (ABE-B) and return-on-investment constraints (ABE-R): computing an exact equilibrium is in FIXP, while computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is in PPAD even for inverse-exponentially small $γ$. At the same time, we establish tight inapproximability for both models: for every fixed $0\leqγ&lt;1$, computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is PPAD-hard, even when all items are displayed and all click-through rates are equal. A key ingredient in our hardness reductions is a new variant of PureCircuit with bounded forward span, which we prove remains PPAD-hard. This structural restriction makes our reduction parameters independent of circuit size, enabling hardness for every fixed $γ&lt;1$. More broadly, our approach suggests a strategy for strengthening PPAD-hardness results in other settings, by developing structural insights into the target problem and new hardness-preserving restrictions on PureCircuit in tandem, with each informing the other and the design of the reduction.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Much Effort Does an AI Code Reviewer Need? Evidence from Claude 5.5 Models on Plausible-but-Wrong Patches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33639v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI coding agents write code faster than people can review it, so teams add AI reviewers. Reasoning models let such a reviewer trade cost for diligence through an effort setting. We asked how much effort a reviewer needs to catch plausible-but-wrong patches, which pass a benchmark's tests but fail stronger ones. Claude Opus 5.5 (preregistered), Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 (exploratory) each reviewed the same 90 agent-written Django patches from SWE-bench Verified at low, medium and high effort. By strengthened SWE-ABS tests, 55 patches were wrong and 35 correct. In every model, high effort accepted fewer wrong patches than low (false success .45 against .67 for Opus, .49 against .65 for Sonnet, .51 against .78 for Haiku), a supported difference for Opus and Haiku. For Opus, medium was non-inferior to high within 15 percentage points (difference .018, 95% CI [-.065, .108]) and cost 20% less, short of the preregistered 30%. For Sonnet and Haiku, medium cost 39% and 51% less than high but was not shown non-inferior. Opus at medium or high agreed best with the labels; Haiku at high cost about 8% as much per review as Opus at medium, accuracy difference unresolved. Exploratory coding showed that most rejections named the tested defect and that effort added such rejections. No setting is a strong gate: each accepted at least 45% of wrong patches. For 32 of the 55 wrong patches the issue may not require what the failing test checks; without them, neither accuracy hypothesis holds for any model.</p></details> | 18 pages, 7 figures. Preregistered at https://osf.io/r8q2m. Data and code: https://github.com/trustmybot/research-artifacts |
| **[Patient-Zero: Scaling Synthetic Patient Agents to Real-World Distributions without Real Patient Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11078v3)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synthetic data generation with Large Language Models (LLMs) has emerged as a promising solution in the medical domain to mitigate data scarcity and privacy constraints. However, existing approaches remain constrained by their derivative nature, relying on real-world records, which pose privacy risks and distribution biases. Furthermore, current patient agents face the Stability-Plasticity Dilemma, struggling to maintain clinical consistency during dynamic inquiries. To address these challenges, we introduce Patient-Zero, a novel framework for ab initio patient simulation that requires no real medical records. Our Medically-Aligned Hierarchical Synthesis framework generates comprehensive and diverse patient records from medical knowledge via stratified attribute permutation. To support rigorous clinical interaction, we design a Dual-Track Cognitive Memory System to enable agents dynamically update memory while preserving logical consistency and persona adherence. Extensive evaluations show that Patient-Zero outperforms baselines in both data quality and interaction fidelity. In physician evaluations, our records obtain the highest mean diversity, consistency, and completeness ratings. Downstream medical reasoning model trained on our synthetic dataset shows substantial performance gains (MedQA +24.0%; MMLU +14.5%), demonstrating the practical utility of our framework.</p></details> | 27 pages, 5 figures. Published in EMNLP 2026 |
| **[WildAnySplat: Feed-Forward 3D Gaussian Splatting in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31023v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present WildAnySplat, a feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting framework that enables robust novel-view synthesis from in-the-wild multi-view images. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. | **[PaveSplat: Resolution-Robust Feed-Forward Gaussian Splatting with Residually Corrected Surface-Aligned Covariances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26045v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present PaveSplat, a feed-forward Gaussian splatting method that adds a learned residual to the analytic surface-aligned covariance, keeping surfels paving the visible surface across rendering resolutions. | **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[GeoGSplat: Geometry-Grounded Non-Pixel-Aligned Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36760v2)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward Gaussian Splatting enables efficient novel view synthesis, but pixel-aligned prediction makes the primitive count grow with image resolution and view count, leading to substantial redundancy and increased computational cost. Non-pixel-aligned methods address this limitation by predicting a fixed number of Gaussians. Without explicit ray and depth constraints, these methods yield misplaced Gaussian centers, causing rendering artifacts under large viewpoint changes. To address this issue, we introduce GeoGSplat, a geometry-grounded feed-forward model that explicitly incorporates geometric priors into the non-pixel-aligned framework. A dual-branch encoder constructs Anchors and Triplane to capture coarse 3D structure and fine-grained scene features, respectively. Subsequently, a hierarchical decoder progressively decodes dense 3D points and Gaussian primitives, enabling 3D reconstruction and novel view synthesis. GeoGSplat surpasses both state-of-the-art pixel-aligned and non-pixel-aligned Gaussian Splatting methods on DTU and NRGBD in terms of novel view synthesis and reconstruction. The advantage of our geometry-grounded paradigm further increases as target viewpoints move farther from the input cameras, demonstrating stronger generalization to large viewpoint changes. Project Page: https://comedy0913.github.io/projects/geogsplat</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |

## pose-free 3D reconstruction
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Deterministic Verification and Computation of Real Matrix Products Have the Same Arithmetic Exponent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34814v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given three $n\times n$ real matrices $A,B,C$, we ask whether $AB=C$. In an exact real arithmetic model that charges one step for each addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, comparison, or sign test, we prove that deterministic exact verification requires $Ω(n^{ω_{\mathbb{C}}})$ steps and has the same optimal arithmetic exponent $ω_{\mathbb{C}}$ as matrix multiplication. Assuming the value $ω_{\mathbb{C}}=9/4$ and the fixed-size border-rank lower bound stated in the OpenAI preprint of November 24, 2026, deterministic verification requires $Ω(n^{9/4})$ steps, whereas Freivalds' randomized test uses $O(n^2)$ steps with one-sided error. More generally, if a tree of cost $s$ correctly decides $y=T(u,v)$ on all real inputs, the bilinear map $T$ is a coefficientwise limit of tensors of real rank $O(s)$.</p></details> | The core proof was developed using generative AI. See the manuscript for details. Independent verification and collaboration on a revised version are welcome |
| **[Complexity of Autobidding Equilibria in GSP Sponsored Shopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13716v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We revisit autobidding equilibria in GSP sponsored shopping auctions, recently proposed and studied in Dütting et al. We establish computational upper bounds for both budget constraints (ABE-B) and return-on-investment constraints (ABE-R): computing an exact equilibrium is in FIXP, while computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is in PPAD even for inverse-exponentially small $γ$. At the same time, we establish tight inapproximability for both models: for every fixed $0\leqγ&lt;1$, computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is PPAD-hard, even when all items are displayed and all click-through rates are equal. A key ingredient in our hardness reductions is a new variant of PureCircuit with bounded forward span, which we prove remains PPAD-hard. This structural restriction makes our reduction parameters independent of circuit size, enabling hardness for every fixed $γ&lt;1$. More broadly, our approach suggests a strategy for strengthening PPAD-hardness results in other settings, by developing structural insights into the target problem and new hardness-preserving restrictions on PureCircuit in tandem, with each informing the other and the design of the reduction.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Much Effort Does an AI Code Reviewer Need? Evidence from Claude 5.5 Models on Plausible-but-Wrong Patches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33639v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI coding agents write code faster than people can review it, so teams add AI reviewers. Reasoning models let such a reviewer trade cost for diligence through an effort setting. We asked how much effort a reviewer needs to catch plausible-but-wrong patches, which pass a benchmark's tests but fail stronger ones. Claude Opus 5.5 (preregistered), Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 (exploratory) each reviewed the same 90 agent-written Django patches from SWE-bench Verified at low, medium and high effort. By strengthened SWE-ABS tests, 55 patches were wrong and 35 correct. In every model, high effort accepted fewer wrong patches than low (false success .45 against .67 for Opus, .49 against .65 for Sonnet, .51 against .78 for Haiku), a supported difference for Opus and Haiku. For Opus, medium was non-inferior to high within 15 percentage points (difference .018, 95% CI [-.065, .108]) and cost 20% less, short of the preregistered 30%. For Sonnet and Haiku, medium cost 39% and 51% less than high but was not shown non-inferior. Opus at medium or high agreed best with the labels; Haiku at high cost about 8% as much per review as Opus at medium, accuracy difference unresolved. Exploratory coding showed that most rejections named the tested defect and that effort added such rejections. No setting is a strong gate: each accepted at least 45% of wrong patches. For 32 of the 55 wrong patches the issue may not require what the failing test checks; without them, neither accuracy hypothesis holds for any model.</p></details> | 18 pages, 7 figures. Preregistered at https://osf.io/r8q2m. Data and code: https://github.com/trustmybot/research-artifacts |
| **[Patient-Zero: Scaling Synthetic Patient Agents to Real-World Distributions without Real Patient Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11078v3)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synthetic data generation with Large Language Models (LLMs) has emerged as a promising solution in the medical domain to mitigate data scarcity and privacy constraints. However, existing approaches remain constrained by their derivative nature, relying on real-world records, which pose privacy risks and distribution biases. Furthermore, current patient agents face the Stability-Plasticity Dilemma, struggling to maintain clinical consistency during dynamic inquiries. To address these challenges, we introduce Patient-Zero, a novel framework for ab initio patient simulation that requires no real medical records. Our Medically-Aligned Hierarchical Synthesis framework generates comprehensive and diverse patient records from medical knowledge via stratified attribute permutation. To support rigorous clinical interaction, we design a Dual-Track Cognitive Memory System to enable agents dynamically update memory while preserving logical consistency and persona adherence. Extensive evaluations show that Patient-Zero outperforms baselines in both data quality and interaction fidelity. In physician evaluations, our records obtain the highest mean diversity, consistency, and completeness ratings. Downstream medical reasoning model trained on our synthetic dataset shows substantial performance gains (MedQA +24.0%; MMLU +14.5%), demonstrating the practical utility of our framework.</p></details> | 27 pages, 5 figures. Published in EMNLP 2026 |
| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Computing $ab+cd$ and other expressions with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30415v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce two ``high-level'' algorithms that make it possible to evaluate the sum $(x\_h+x\_\ell) + (y\_h+y\_\ell)$ of two double-word numbers with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic. These algorithms allow one to evaluate expressions of the form $ab+cd$, $a+b+cd$, $a+b+c+d$, and $abc$ with correct rounding, where $a$, $b$, $c$, and $d$ are floating-point numbers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[Sharp Volume Contraction of a Centrally Cut John Ellipsoid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29323v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ in positive dimension, let $w(K)$ be the volume of its maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. For a closed halfspace $H$ whose boundary passes through the center of that ellipsoid, we prove \[ w(K\cap H)\le R_nw(K),\qquad R_1=\frac12,\qquad R_n=\frac12\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}\quad(n\ge2), \] with equality attained in every fixed dimension. This refines the optimal dimension-independent bound from the author's earlier work on [Khachiyan's ellipsoid conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28447). We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |
| **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. A compact predictor learns reference-relative disagreement (RRD) between each candidate's depth prediction and the reference prediction using regression and ranking losses. | **[Improved Accuracy Scaling with Problem Complexity in LLM Arithmetic Reasoning Using Executable Symbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27203v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High accuracy on mathematical benchmarks does not establish whether reasoning remains reliable as problem complexity increases. We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. | **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Indyk and Xu introduced the notion of $α$-reachable graphs, which include graphs built by DiskANN's slow-preprocessing construction, and proved that greedy search achieves an approximation factor of $\tfrac{α+1}{α-1}$. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). | **[Enhancing Sparse-View Gaussian Splatting with Feed-Forward Priors via Spectral Fusion and Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31428v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Gaussian splatting from sparse training views renders high-quality images near training views, but reconstruction quality degrades at distant regions, which are undersampled by the training set. Our key idea is then to intelligently fuse the reliable part of each of the two methods. To remove the slow feed-forward model at render time, we use these fused images as teachers and distill them back into Gaussians, balanced with supervision from input views. Our method improves the quality of various sparse-view Gaussian methods without sacrificing rendering speed, and our analysis works on different prior models. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |

## large-scale Gaussian Splatting
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Deterministic Verification and Computation of Real Matrix Products Have the Same Arithmetic Exponent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34814v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given three $n\times n$ real matrices $A,B,C$, we ask whether $AB=C$. Assuming the value $ω_{\mathbb{C}}=9/4$ and the fixed-size border-rank lower bound stated in the OpenAI preprint of November 24, 2026, deterministic verification requires $Ω(n^{9/4})$ steps, whereas Freivalds' randomized test uses $O(n^2)$ steps with one-sided error. More generally, if a tree of cost $s$ correctly decides $y=T(u,v)$ on all real inputs, the bilinear map $T$ is a coefficientwise limit of tensors of real rank $O(s)$.</p></details> | The core proof was developed using generative AI. See the manuscript for details. Independent verification and collaboration on a revised version are welcome |
| **[Large-Scale Gaussian Splatting with a Multi-GPU PyTorch Backend](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11390v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Gaussian splatting methods have become increasingly popular for neural reconstruction of the real world. However, they are often limited in scale and resolution due to compute and memory constraints. We present a multi-GPU Gaussian splatting approach that scales reconstruction to higher resolutions and larger scenes while abstracting away the code complexity typically associated with distributing a model. We demonstrate city-scale reconstructions with street-level detail consisting of over 1 billion Gaussian splats. To accomplish this, we propose a PyTorch backend that distributes parameters and operators across GPUs via CUDA unified memory, with optional NVLink support. Because distribution occurs at the operator level, the model code requires no explicit cross-device communication. More broadly, the backend exposes multiple GPUs as an aggregate asynchronous PyTorch device and supports a broad set of PyTorch operators enabling the distribution of general PyTorch workloads.</p></details> | 13 pages, 8 tables, 4 figures, and 1 listing. Includes supplementary material |
| **[Complexity of Autobidding Equilibria in GSP Sponsored Shopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13716v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We revisit autobidding equilibria in GSP sponsored shopping auctions, recently proposed and studied in Dütting et al. We establish computational upper bounds for both budget constraints (ABE-B) and return-on-investment constraints (ABE-R): computing an exact equilibrium is in FIXP, while computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is in PPAD even for inverse-exponentially small $γ$. At the same time, we establish tight inapproximability for both models: for every fixed $0\leqγ&lt;1$, computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is PPAD-hard, even when all items are displayed and all click-through rates are equal. A key ingredient in our hardness reductions is a new variant of PureCircuit with bounded forward span, which we prove remains PPAD-hard. This structural restriction makes our reduction parameters independent of circuit size, enabling hardness for every fixed $γ&lt;1$. More broadly, our approach suggests a strategy for strengthening PPAD-hardness results in other settings, by developing structural insights into the target problem and new hardness-preserving restrictions on PureCircuit in tandem, with each informing the other and the design of the reduction.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Much Effort Does an AI Code Reviewer Need? Evidence from Claude 5.5 Models on Plausible-but-Wrong Patches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33639v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI coding agents write code faster than people can review it, so teams add AI reviewers. Reasoning models let such a reviewer trade cost for diligence through an effort setting. We asked how much effort a reviewer needs to catch plausible-but-wrong patches, which pass a benchmark's tests but fail stronger ones. Claude Opus 5.5 (preregistered), Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 (exploratory) each reviewed the same 90 agent-written Django patches from SWE-bench Verified at low, medium and high effort. By strengthened SWE-ABS tests, 55 patches were wrong and 35 correct. In every model, high effort accepted fewer wrong patches than low (false success .45 against .67 for Opus, .49 against .65 for Sonnet, .51 against .78 for Haiku), a supported difference for Opus and Haiku. For Opus, medium was non-inferior to high within 15 percentage points (difference .018, 95% CI [-.065, .108]) and cost 20% less, short of the preregistered 30%. For Sonnet and Haiku, medium cost 39% and 51% less than high but was not shown non-inferior. Opus at medium or high agreed best with the labels; Haiku at high cost about 8% as much per review as Opus at medium, accuracy difference unresolved. Exploratory coding showed that most rejections named the tested defect and that effort added such rejections. No setting is a strong gate: each accepted at least 45% of wrong patches. For 32 of the 55 wrong patches the issue may not require what the failing test checks; without them, neither accuracy hypothesis holds for any model.</p></details> | 18 pages, 7 figures. Preregistered at https://osf.io/r8q2m. Data and code: https://github.com/trustmybot/research-artifacts |

For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |

| **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Independently, Al-Jazzazi et al. proposed adaptive beam search (ABS) with a relaxation parameter $γ&gt; 0$, showing a $\tfrac{2}γ$ approximation on navigable graphs ($α\approx 1$), where greedy search admits no guarantees. In this work, we unify and generalize these results by proving that ABS with parameter $γ$ on $α$-reachable graphs achieves an approximation of $\tfrac{α+1}{α(γ+1)-1}$ for any metric. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |

## dynamic Gaussian Splatting
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[A Mesh for Every Moment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34939v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present MEMO (a Mesh for Every MOment), a method that reconstructs the geometry of complete dynamic scenes, captured by multiple synchronized cameras, and extracts a mesh at every frame. Existing dynamic Gaussian splatting methods either model appearance alone or target isolated objects segmented by foreground masks, and all of them recover meshes by TSDF fusion or Poisson reconstruction, whose fixed grids limit the detail they can capture. At the core of MEMO is a per-Gaussian motion model inspired by Gaussian processes, which represents each Gaussian's position and rotation over time as a combination of temporal Gaussian kernels. The model keeps every Gaussian a valid surface element throughout the sequence and carries an implicit representation of the dynamic geometry, so a mesh can be derived at any time from a field defined by the Gaussians. Although common in real captures, large and fast motion poses a challenge that dynamic Gaussian splatting methods usually overlook. To account for it, we design a track-based initialization scheme that fits each Gaussian's initial trajectory to point tracks in closed form. We evaluate MEMO on Blender-Moments, a new benchmark of nine animated scenes with ground-truth meshes at every frame, and on two real-world datasets. MEMO reconstructs the most accurate geometry among state-of-the-art methods, and remains competitive in novel-view synthesis and training time. Project page: https://memo-4d.github.io</p></details> | Project page: https://memo-4d.github.io |
| **[Deterministic Verification and Computation of Real Matrix Products Have the Same Arithmetic Exponent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34814v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given three $n\times n$ real matrices $A,B,C$, we ask whether $AB=C$. Assuming the value $ω_{\mathbb{C}}=9/4$ and the fixed-size border-rank lower bound stated in the OpenAI preprint of November 24, 2026, deterministic verification requires $Ω(n^{9/4})$ steps, whereas Freivalds' randomized test uses $O(n^2)$ steps with one-sided error. More generally, if a tree of cost $s$ correctly decides $y=T(u,v)$ on all real inputs, the bilinear map $T$ is a coefficientwise limit of tensors of real rank $O(s)$.</p></details> | The core proof was developed using generative AI. See the manuscript for details. Independent verification and collaboration on a revised version are welcome |

We present AnyCam4D, which trains native 4D Gaussians on original footage by ray-based rasterization, preserving field of view and resolution at about the per-step cost of standard projection. Our key contribution is a numerically stable way to obtain orientation and size: we condition a square root of the 4D covariance. Trained models render through other cameras, with rolling shutter, depth of field or chromatic aberration. With a synthetic 170-degree fisheye camera, training on original images improves peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) by 3.0-8.1 dB. On real footage, AnyCam4D has lower perceptual error than the standard projection adapted to each camera, at comparable PSNR and without camera-specific derivations. With the synthetic camera rotated 60-80 degrees away from the capture rig's view, it leads by 6.2 dB against ground truth.</p></details> | Project page: https://sascha-kirch.github.io/anycam4d/ |

| **[CueSplat: Learning to Schedule Sparse Deformation for Dynamic Gaussian Splatting on Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22095v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deformation-based 4D Gaussian splatting (4DGS) enables dynamic scene rendering. Specifically, CueSplat predicts Gaussian attributes from recent history and uses a lightweight learned router to select exact deformation updates within a scene-specific budget calibrated offline. To control accumulated prediction error, we use drift signals to schedule three consecutive full-deformation frames that rebuild the prediction history. Main paper only |
| **[HPC: Hierarchical Point-based Latent Representation for Streaming Dynamic Gaussian Splatting Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00671v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While dynamic Gaussian Splatting has driven significant advances in free-viewpoint video, maintaining its rendering quality with a small memory footprint for efficient streaming transmission still presents an ongoing challenge. It employs a hierarchical point-based latent representation that operates on a per-Gaussian basis to avoid parameter redundancy in unoccupied space. To improve compression efficiency, we further undertake the first investigation to compress neural networks for streaming dynamic Gaussian Splatting through mining and exploiting the inter-frame correlation of parameters. Combined with latent compression, this forms a fully end-to-end compression framework. Comprehensive experimental evaluations demonstrate that HPC substantially outperforms state-of-the-art methods. It achieves a storage reduction of 67% against its baseline while maintaining high reconstruction fidelity.</p></details> | Code available at: https://github.com/USTC-iVC-3D-Group/HPC |

| **[FOCI: Trajectory Optimization on Gaussian Splats for Gaussian Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33380v1)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has recently gained popularity as a faster alternative to Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs) in 3D reconstruction and view synthesis methods. We extensively test our algorithm in both synthetic and real Gaussian Splats, showcasing that collision-free trajectories for legged, aerial and manipulator robots can be computed in seconds, even with hundreds of thousands of Gaussians making up the environment.
