| **[VersionGS: Compact and Motion-Robust Dynamic Gaussian Splatting via Versioned Gaussians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33374v1)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent dynamic Gaussian splatting methods encode temporal change, either as smooth functions of time or as constrained updates between frames. | **[UniView: Large-Baseline View Synthesis via Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04701v3)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Novel view synthesis from casually captured monocular images and videos offers a practical route to immersive content creation without dedicated multi-view capture. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. | **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. In this work, we unify and generalize these results by proving that ABS with parameter $γ$ on $α$-reachable graphs achieves an approximation of $\tfrac{α+1}{α(γ+1)-1}$ for any metric. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. ## Gaussian Splatting SLAM
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Deterministic Verification and Computation of Real Matrix Products Have the Same Arithmetic Exponent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34814v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given three $n\times n$ real matrices $A,B,C$, we ask whether $AB=C$. In an exact real arithmetic model that charges one step for each addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, comparison, or sign test, we prove that deterministic exact verification requires $Ω(n^{ω_{\mathbb{C}}})$ steps and has the same optimal arithmetic exponent $ω_{\mathbb{C}}$ as matrix multiplication. Assuming the value $ω_{\mathbb{C}}=9/4$ and the fixed-size border-rank lower bound stated in the OpenAI preprint of November 24, 2026, deterministic verification requires $Ω(n^{9/4})$ steps, whereas Freivalds' randomized test uses $O(n^2)$ steps with one-sided error. More generally, if a tree of cost $s$ correctly decides $y=T(u,v)$ on all real inputs, the bilinear map $T$ is a coefficientwise limit of tensors of real rank $O(s)$.</p></details> | The core proof was developed using generative AI. See the manuscript for details. Independent verification and collaboration on a revised version are welcome |

It performs tracking and mapping in the spherical domain and uses tracked visual features in regions without LiDAR coverage to improve pose estimation. To address insufficient coverage of newly observed surfaces and floating artifacts during incremental mapping, we introduce Depth-Aware Map Extension and Pruning (DAEP). To enable broader evaluation, we collect a new dataset, WHOLE, using a UAV platform across Weak-geometry, High-dynamic, Omnidirectional, Low-light, and Easy scenarios. Extensive experiments on public benchmarks and WHOLE demonstrate competitive rendering quality and improved geometric accuracy relative to perspective systems. Compared with existing panoramic baselines, GOLD-SLAM improves mean PSNR by 5.56-9.10 dB and reduces mean Depth-L1 by 71.9-83.9%. | **[How Much Effort Does an AI Code Reviewer Need? By strengthened SWE-ABS tests, 55 patches were wrong and 35 correct. In every model, high effort accepted fewer wrong patches than low (false success .45 against .67 for Opus, .49 against .65 for Sonnet, .51 against .78 for Haiku), a supported difference for Opus and Haiku. For Opus, medium was non-inferior to high within 15 percentage points (difference .018, 95% CI [-.065, .108]) and cost 20% less, short of the preregistered 30%. For Sonnet and Haiku, medium cost 39% and 51% less than high but was not shown non-inferior. Opus at medium or high agreed best with the labels; Haiku at high cost about 8% as much per review as Opus at medium, accuracy difference unresolved. Exploratory coding showed that most rejections named the tested defect and that effort added such rejections. No setting is a strong gate: each accepted at least 45% of wrong patches. | **[CoSphere-SLAM: Multi-Agent Panoramic Gaussian Splatting SLAM with Spherical Loop Closure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22235v2)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dense collaborative mapping is essential for search and rescue, building inspection, and warehouse automation, where panoramic cameras capture the entire surroundings in a single frame. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |

In an exact real arithmetic model that charges one step for each addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, comparison, or sign test, we prove that deterministic exact verification requires $Ω(n^{ω_{\mathbb{C}}})$ steps and has the same optimal arithmetic exponent $ω_{\mathbb{C}}$ as matrix multiplication. Assuming the value $ω_{\mathbb{C}}=9/4$ and the fixed-size border-rank lower bound stated in the OpenAI preprint of November 24, 2026, deterministic verification requires $Ω(n^{9/4})$ steps, whereas Freivalds' randomized test uses $O(n^2)$ steps with one-sided error. More generally, if a tree of cost $s$ correctly decides $y=T(u,v)$ on all real inputs, the bilinear map $T$ is a coefficientwise limit of tensors of real rank $O(s)$.</p></details> | The core proof was developed using generative AI. See the manuscript for details. Independent verification and collaboration on a revised version are welcome |

We establish computational upper bounds for both budget constraints (ABE-B) and return-on-investment constraints (ABE-R): computing an exact equilibrium is in FIXP, while computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is in PPAD even for inverse-exponentially small $γ$. At the same time, we establish tight inapproximability for both models: for every fixed $0\leqγ&lt;1$, computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is PPAD-hard, even when all items are displayed and all click-through rates are equal. A key ingredient in our hardness reductions is a new variant of PureCircuit with bounded forward span, which we prove remains PPAD-hard. This structural restriction makes our reduction parameters independent of circuit size, enabling hardness for every fixed $γ&lt;1$. More broadly, our approach suggests a strategy for strengthening PPAD-hardness results in other settings, by developing structural insights into the target problem and new hardness-preserving restrictions on PureCircuit in tandem, with each informing the other and the design of the reduction.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Much Effort Does an AI Code Reviewer Need? Evidence from Claude 5.5 Models on Plausible-but-Wrong Patches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33639v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI coding agents write code faster than people can review it, so teams add AI reviewers. Reasoning models let such a reviewer trade cost for diligence through an effort setting. We asked how much effort a reviewer needs to catch plausible-but-wrong patches, which pass a benchmark's tests but fail stronger ones. Claude Opus 5.5 (preregistered), Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 (exploratory) each reviewed the same 90 agent-written Django patches from SWE-bench Verified at low, medium and high effort. By strengthened SWE-ABS tests, 55 patches were wrong and 35 correct. In every model, high effort accepted fewer wrong patches than low (false success .45 against .67 for Opus, .49 against .65 for Sonnet, .51 against .78 for Haiku), a supported difference for Opus and Haiku. For Opus, medium was non-inferior to high within 15 percentage points (difference .018, 95% CI [-.065, .108]) and cost 20% less, short of the preregistered 30%. For Sonnet and Haiku, medium cost 39% and 51% less than high but was not shown non-inferior. Opus at medium or high agreed best with the labels; Haiku at high cost about 8% as much per review as Opus at medium, accuracy difference unresolved. Exploratory coding showed that most rejections named the tested defect and that effort added such rejections. No setting is a strong gate: each accepted at least 45% of wrong patches. For 32 of the 55 wrong patches the issue may not require what the failing test checks; without them, neither accuracy hypothesis holds for any model.</p></details> | 18 pages, 7 figures. To address these challenges, we introduce Patient-Zero, a novel framework for ab initio patient simulation that requires no real medical records. Our Medically-Aligned Hierarchical Synthesis framework generates comprehensive and diverse patient records from medical knowledge via stratified attribute permutation. To support rigorous clinical interaction, we design a Dual-Track Cognitive Memory System to enable agents dynamically update memory while preserving logical consistency and persona adherence. Extensive evaluations show that Patient-Zero outperforms baselines in both data quality and interaction fidelity. In physician evaluations, our records obtain the highest mean diversity, consistency, and completeness ratings. Downstream medical reasoning model trained on our synthetic dataset shows substantial performance gains (MedQA +24.0%; MMLU +14.5%), demonstrating the practical utility of our framework.</p></details> | 27 pages, 5 figures. | **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. | **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. | **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. | **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |

| **[Deterministic Verification and Computation of Real Matrix Products Have the Same Arithmetic Exponent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34814v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given three $n\times n$ real matrices $A,B,C$, we ask whether $AB=C$. In an exact real arithmetic model that charges one step for each addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, comparison, or sign test, we prove that deterministic exact verification requires $Ω(n^{ω_{\mathbb{C}}})$ steps and has the same optimal arithmetic exponent $ω_{\mathbb{C}}$ as matrix multiplication. Assuming the value $ω_{\mathbb{C}}=9/4$ and the fixed-size border-rank lower bound stated in the OpenAI preprint of November 24, 2026, deterministic verification requires $Ω(n^{9/4})$ steps, whereas Freivalds' randomized test uses $O(n^2)$ steps with one-sided error. More generally, if a tree of cost $s$ correctly decides $y=T(u,v)$ on all real inputs, the bilinear map $T$ is a coefficientwise limit of tensors of real rank $O(s)$.</p></details> | The core proof was developed using generative AI. See the manuscript for details. Independent verification and collaboration on a revised version are welcome |
| **[Complexity of Autobidding Equilibria in GSP Sponsored Shopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13716v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We revisit autobidding equilibria in GSP sponsored shopping auctions, recently proposed and studied in Dütting et al. We establish computational upper bounds for both budget constraints (ABE-B) and return-on-investment constraints (ABE-R): computing an exact equilibrium is in FIXP, while computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is in PPAD even for inverse-exponentially small $γ$. At the same time, we establish tight inapproximability for both models: for every fixed $0\leqγ&lt;1$, computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is PPAD-hard, even when all items are displayed and all click-through rates are equal. A key ingredient in our hardness reductions is a new variant of PureCircuit with bounded forward span, which we prove remains PPAD-hard. This structural restriction makes our reduction parameters independent of circuit size, enabling hardness for every fixed $γ&lt;1$. More broadly, our approach suggests a strategy for strengthening PPAD-hardness results in other settings, by developing structural insights into the target problem and new hardness-preserving restrictions on PureCircuit in tandem, with each informing the other and the design of the reduction.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Much Effort Does an AI Code Reviewer Need? Evidence from Claude 5.5 Models on Plausible-but-Wrong Patches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33639v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI coding agents write code faster than people can review it, so teams add AI reviewers. Reasoning models let such a reviewer trade cost for diligence through an effort setting. We asked how much effort a reviewer needs to catch plausible-but-wrong patches, which pass a benchmark's tests but fail stronger ones. Claude Opus 5.5 (preregistered), Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 (exploratory) each reviewed the same 90 agent-written Django patches from SWE-bench Verified at low, medium and high effort. By strengthened SWE-ABS tests, 55 patches were wrong and 35 correct. In every model, high effort accepted fewer wrong patches than low (false success .45 against .67 for Opus, .49 against .65 for Sonnet, .51 against .78 for Haiku), a supported difference for Opus and Haiku. For Opus, medium was non-inferior to high within 15 percentage points (difference .018, 95% CI [-.065, .108]) and cost 20% less, short of the preregistered 30%. For Sonnet and Haiku, medium cost 39% and 51% less than high but was not shown non-inferior. Opus at medium or high agreed best with the labels; Haiku at high cost about 8% as much per review as Opus at medium, accuracy difference unresolved. Exploratory coding showed that most rejections named the tested defect and that effort added such rejections. No setting is a strong gate: each accepted at least 45% of wrong patches. For 32 of the 55 wrong patches the issue may not require what the failing test checks; without them, neither accuracy hypothesis holds for any model.</p></details> | 18 pages, 7 figures. | **[Patient-Zero: Scaling Synthetic Patient Agents to Real-World Distributions without Real Patient Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11078v3)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synthetic data generation with Large Language Models (LLMs) has emerged as a promising solution in the medical domain to mitigate data scarcity and privacy constraints. Furthermore, current patient agents face the Stability-Plasticity Dilemma, struggling to maintain clinical consistency during dynamic inquiries. To address these challenges, we introduce Patient-Zero, a novel framework for ab initio patient simulation that requires no real medical records. Our Medically-Aligned Hierarchical Synthesis framework generates comprehensive and diverse patient records from medical knowledge via stratified attribute permutation. To support rigorous clinical interaction, we design a Dual-Track Cognitive Memory System to enable agents dynamically update memory while preserving logical consistency and persona adherence. Published in EMNLP 2026 |
| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Computing $ab+cd$ and other expressions with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30415v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce two ``high-level'' algorithms that make it possible to evaluate the sum $(x\_h+x\_\ell) + (y\_h+y\_\ell)$ of two double-word numbers with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic. These algorithms allow one to evaluate expressions of the form $ab+cd$, $a+b+cd$, $a+b+c+d$, and $abc$ with correct rounding, where $a$, $b$, $c$, and $d$ are floating-point numbers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[Sharp Volume Contraction of a Centrally Cut John Ellipsoid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29323v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ in positive dimension, let $w(K)$ be the volume of its maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. For a closed halfspace $H$ whose boundary passes through the center of that ellipsoid, we prove \[ w(K\cap H)\le R_nw(K),\qquad R_1=\frac12,\qquad R_n=\frac12\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}\quad(n\ge2), \] with equality attained in every fixed dimension. This refines the optimal dimension-independent bound from the author's earlier work on [Khachiyan's ellipsoid conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28447). We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |
| **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur. We present ADEPT, a framework that adapts these settings using learned feedback from a frozen depth model. During training, ground-truth depth identifies the lowest-error reference within a group of physically evaluated camera settings. A compact predictor learns reference-relative disagreement (RRD) between each candidate's depth prediction and the reference prediction using regression and ranking losses. At deployment, it scores observed captures using frozen-model features and camera, motion, and illumination context, without ground truth or reference images. A contextual Gaussian-process controller uses recent scores to select subsequent exposure and gain settings under motion and photometric constraints. We evaluate offline camera selection with relative and metric Depth Anything v2 and demonstrate online control with the affine-invariant variant on a mobile robot. Across nine equally weighted scene-illumination conditions, ADEPT reduces mean scale-and-shift-aligned $\mathrm{Abs}_{\mathrm{rel}}$ by 14.6% relative to built-in auto-exposure and by 10.0% relative to the best-performing evaluated prior controller.</p></details> |  |
| **[Improved Accuracy Scaling with Problem Complexity in LLM Arithmetic Reasoning Using Executable Symbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27203v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High accuracy on mathematical benchmarks does not establish whether reasoning remains reliable as problem complexity increases. We examine how executable symbolic representations change accuracy scaling in large language models compared with natural-language reasoning. To address this limitation, we introduce the GSM Arithmetic Benchmark (GSM-AB), a controlled benchmark that independently varies operand digit length and operation count to probe numerical scale and compositional complexity. We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. A Python program-of-thought (Python-PoT) control shows a similar operation-axis pattern, suggesting that this finding is not specific to Lean. These findings show that reasoning representation shapes arithmetic accuracy scaling and support executable symbolic representations for more reliable reasoning on complex arithmetic tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Indyk and Xu introduced the notion of $α$-reachable graphs, which include graphs built by DiskANN's slow-preprocessing construction, and proved that greedy search achieves an approximation factor of $\tfrac{α+1}{α-1}$. Independently, Al-Jazzazi et al. proposed adaptive beam search (ABS) with a relaxation parameter $γ&gt; 0$, showing a $\tfrac{2}γ$ approximation on navigable graphs ($α\approx 1$), where greedy search admits no guarantees. In this work, we unify and generalize these results by proving that ABS with parameter $γ$ on $α$-reachable graphs achieves an approximation of $\tfrac{α+1}{α(γ+1)-1}$ for any metric. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. In memory, it needs $1.3$--$23.7\%$ fewer distance computations than CBS and $0.0$--$3.9\%$ fewer than ABS.</p></details> | 22 pages, 1 figure, 9 tables |
| **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Operator announcements are distinguished from independent measurements and future plans. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |

We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. A Python program-of-thought (Python-PoT) control shows a similar operation-axis pattern, suggesting that this finding is not specific to Lean. These findings show that reasoning representation shapes arithmetic accuracy scaling and support executable symbolic representations for more reliable reasoning on complex arithmetic tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Variants of Beam Search for DiskANN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26609v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based data structures such as DiskANN, HNSW, and NSG constitute the state of the art for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search. Indyk and Xu introduced the notion of $α$-reachable graphs, which include graphs built by DiskANN's slow-preprocessing construction, and proved that greedy search achieves an approximation factor of $\tfrac{α+1}{α-1}$. Independently, Al-Jazzazi et al. proposed adaptive beam search (ABS) with a relaxation parameter $γ&gt; 0$, showing a $\tfrac{2}γ$ approximation on navigable graphs ($α\approx 1$), where greedy search admits no guarantees. In this work, we unify and generalize these results by proving that ABS with parameter $γ$ on $α$-reachable graphs achieves an approximation of $\tfrac{α+1}{α(γ+1)-1}$ for any metric. Moreover, on sorted $α$-reachable graphs (introduced by Gollapudi et al.) under the $\ell_2$ metric, we obtain the tighter bound $\max\bigl(\tfracα{α(γ+1)-1}, \tfrac{α^{2}+1}{α^{2}(γ+1)-1}\bigr)$. Beyond these theoretical results, we design a beam predictor using features inspired by fine-grained analysis of ABS, trained via a constrained objective that minimizes mean distance computations subject to a target recall. On three 100-million-point benchmarks, using graphs built by DiskANN's practical construction, beam predictor lowers mean query latency on SSD-resident indices at matched recall by up to $29.6\%$ relative to classical beam search (CBS). Relative to ABS, it is faster in $11$ of $15$ settings, by up to $6.5\%$, and at most $1.4\%$ slower in the other $4$; at $95\%$ recall, its advantage over ABS grows from $0.8$--$4.2\%$ for $100$ neighbors to $4.6$--$5.6\%$ for $500$ neighbors. | **[A Brief Overview of Non-Terrestrial Networks (Updated 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25984v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This article extends the 2023 preprint \emph{A brief introduction to satellite communications for Non-Terrestrial Networks (NTN)} (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.04590v1}{arXiv:2305.04590v1}) with a revision of its foundations, tables and state of the art as of 18 November 2026. It revisits propagation, link budgets, spectrum reuse and phased arrays, including corrected equations and numerical examples. Updated tables distinguish operational, launched and planned constellations and compare published fixed-terminal rates with direct-to-device services. The discussion incorporates developments in 3GPP Releases 17--20, commercial LTE-based direct-to-cell services and research towards 6G non-terrestrial networks. Open challenges include spectrum coordination, Doppler and timing compensation, mobility, capacity, security and the sustainability of low Earth orbit.</p></details> |  |
| **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/msalman95/DAL.</p></details> | Accepted at the British Machine Vision Conference (BMVC) 2026. 18 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/msalman95/DA |

## infrared Gaussian Splatting
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Deterministic Verification and Computation of Real Matrix Products Have the Same Arithmetic Exponent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34814v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given three $n\times n$ real matrices $A,B,C$, we ask whether $AB=C$. In an exact real arithmetic model that charges one step for each addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, comparison, or sign test, we prove that deterministic exact verification requires $Ω(n^{ω_{\mathbb{C}}})$ steps and has the same optimal arithmetic exponent $ω_{\mathbb{C}}$ as matrix multiplication. Assuming the value $ω_{\mathbb{C}}=9/4$ and the fixed-size border-rank lower bound stated in the OpenAI preprint of November 24, 2026, deterministic verification requires $Ω(n^{9/4})$ steps, whereas Freivalds' randomized test uses $O(n^2)$ steps with one-sided error. More generally, if a tree of cost $s$ correctly decides $y=T(u,v)$ on all real inputs, the bilinear map $T$ is a coefficientwise limit of tensors of real rank $O(s)$.</p></details> | The core proof was developed using generative AI. See the manuscript for details. Independent verification and collaboration on a revised version are welcome |
| **[Complexity of Autobidding Equilibria in GSP Sponsored Shopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13716v2)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We revisit autobidding equilibria in GSP sponsored shopping auctions, recently proposed and studied in Dütting et al. We establish computational upper bounds for both budget constraints (ABE-B) and return-on-investment constraints (ABE-R): computing an exact equilibrium is in FIXP, while computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is in PPAD even for inverse-exponentially small $γ$. At the same time, we establish tight inapproximability for both models: for every fixed $0\leqγ&lt;1$, computing a $γ$-approximate equilibrium is PPAD-hard, even when all items are displayed and all click-through rates are equal. A key ingredient in our hardness reductions is a new variant of PureCircuit with bounded forward span, which we prove remains PPAD-hard. This structural restriction makes our reduction parameters independent of circuit size, enabling hardness for every fixed $γ&lt;1$. More broadly, our approach suggests a strategy for strengthening PPAD-hardness results in other settings, by developing structural insights into the target problem and new hardness-preserving restrictions on PureCircuit in tandem, with each informing the other and the design of the reduction.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Much Effort Does an AI Code Reviewer Need? Evidence from Claude 5.5 Models on Plausible-but-Wrong Patches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33639v1)** | 2026-11-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI coding agents write code faster than people can review it, so teams add AI reviewers. Reasoning models let such a reviewer trade cost for diligence through an effort setting. We asked how much effort a reviewer needs to catch plausible-but-wrong patches, which pass a benchmark's tests but fail stronger ones. Claude Opus 5.5 (preregistered), Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 (exploratory) each reviewed the same 90 agent-written Django patches from SWE-bench Verified at low, medium and high effort. By strengthened SWE-ABS tests, 55 patches were wrong and 35 correct. In every model, high effort accepted fewer wrong patches than low (false success .45 against .67 for Opus, .49 against .65 for Sonnet, .51 against .78 for Haiku), a supported difference for Opus and Haiku. For Opus, medium was non-inferior to high within 15 percentage points (difference .018, 95% CI [-.065, .108]) and cost 20% less, short of the preregistered 30%. For Sonnet and Haiku, medium cost 39% and 51% less than high but was not shown non-inferior. Opus at medium or high agreed best with the labels; Haiku at high cost about 8% as much per review as Opus at medium, accuracy difference unresolved. Exploratory coding showed that most rejections named the tested defect and that effort added such rejections. No setting is a strong gate: each accepted at least 45% of wrong patches. For 32 of the 55 wrong patches the issue may not require what the failing test checks; without them, neither accuracy hypothesis holds for any model.</p></details> | 18 pages, 7 figures. Preregistered at https://osf.io/r8q2m. Data and code: https://github.com/trustmybot/research-artifacts |
| **[Patient-Zero: Scaling Synthetic Patient Agents to Real-World Distributions without Real Patient Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11078v3)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synthetic data generation with Large Language Models (LLMs) has emerged as a promising solution in the medical domain to mitigate data scarcity and privacy constraints. However, existing approaches remain constrained by their derivative nature, relying on real-world records, which pose privacy risks and distribution biases. Furthermore, current patient agents face the Stability-Plasticity Dilemma, struggling to maintain clinical consistency during dynamic inquiries. To address these challenges, we introduce Patient-Zero, a novel framework for ab initio patient simulation that requires no real medical records. Our Medically-Aligned Hierarchical Synthesis framework generates comprehensive and diverse patient records from medical knowledge via stratified attribute permutation. To support rigorous clinical interaction, we design a Dual-Track Cognitive Memory System to enable agents dynamically update memory while preserving logical consistency and persona adherence. Extensive evaluations show that Patient-Zero outperforms baselines in both data quality and interaction fidelity. In physician evaluations, our records obtain the highest mean diversity, consistency, and completeness ratings. Downstream medical reasoning model trained on our synthetic dataset shows substantial performance gains (MedQA +24.0%; MMLU +14.5%), demonstrating the practical utility of our framework.</p></details> | 27 pages, 5 figures. Published in EMNLP 2026 |
| **[Information-Theoretic Generalization for Adaptive Learning Under Privacy and Fairness Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30867v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive learners may update, select, and stop models after repeatedly examining the same data. Privacy and fairness constraints regulate this adaptation only selectively: they limit information about a private or sensitive coordinate, whereas task generalization depends on information about the coordinate on which loss is evaluated. We develop a unified information-theoretic framework for this mismatch. For a finite-alphabet pair $(A,B)$, we define directly the residual-information certificate $ h_ε(P_{AB})=\min\{H(B),H(B&#124; A)+ε\}. $ A chain-rule transfer inequality converts a bound on $I(W;A)$ into an upper bound on $I(W;B)$, which can be inserted into a mutual-information generalization inequality. We first establish a transcript-level composition theorem for sequential learners. Conditional per-update information budgets add along the transcript, and the resulting certificate remains valid for history-dependent checkpoint selection, bounded stopping, and independent heterogeneous data batches. Under selective privacy, the learner observes $(X^n,Y^n)$, protects $X^n$, and incurs loss on $Y^n$. Under fairness, the learner observes $(S^n,X^n,T^n)$, limits dependence on the sensitive block $S^n$, compresses $X^n$, and incurs loss on $T^n$. The bounds expose the residual entropies $H(Y&#124; X)$, $H(T&#124; S)$, and $H(T&#124; X)$ and the cumulative information cost of adaptation. We retain block and individual-sample bounds, task-compression refinements, per-output total-variation privacy, empirical-to-population demographic-parity transfer, and finite-alphabet information-parity transfer. FRL/EFRL/SFRL/ESFRL constructions study the tightness of the task-information upper bounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Computing $ab+cd$ and other expressions with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30415v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce two ``high-level'' algorithms that make it possible to evaluate the sum $(x\_h+x\_\ell) + (y\_h+y\_\ell)$ of two double-word numbers with correct rounding in binary floating-point arithmetic. These algorithms allow one to evaluate expressions of the form $ab+cd$, $a+b+cd$, $a+b+c+d$, and $abc$ with correct rounding, where $a$, $b$, $c$, and $d$ are floating-point numbers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[Sharp Volume Contraction of a Centrally Cut John Ellipsoid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29323v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ in positive dimension, let $w(K)$ be the volume of its maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. For a closed halfspace $H$ whose boundary passes through the center of that ellipsoid, we prove \[ w(K\cap H)\le R_nw(K),\qquad R_1=\frac12,\qquad R_n=\frac12\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^{(n-1)/2}\quad(n\ge2), \] with equality attained in every fixed dimension. This refines the optimal dimension-independent bound from the author's earlier work on [Khachiyan's ellipsoid conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28447). We characterize the full equality family, including nonsimplex extremizers, and establish an exact volume-deficit identity that yields dimension-independent stability of the new ellipsoid and its weighted contact geometry in John coordinates. The proof reduces the problem to finite John contacts and constructs an explicit conic certificate by a transformation of contact directions and a common polar rotation. Its determinant is controlled by a nonnegative contact defect. The sharp contraction theorem, equality classification, stability estimates, and supporting results have been formally verified in Lean 4 using mathlib.</p></details> | Lean 4 formalization with mathlib, see [github repository](https://github.com/DrZhouKarl/SharpContractionCutJohnEllipsoid) |
| **[ADEPT: Adaptive Monocular Depth Estimation via Model-In-The-Loop Photometric Tuning with Capture Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27423v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure and gain determine the visual evidence available to monocular depth estimation (MDE), particularly when robot motion creates a tradeoff between light collection and motion blur.
